#5127. Top 12 Crypto Debit Cards to Consider

Home » Cryptopedia » Top 12 Crypto Debit Cards to Consider

Compare fees, cashback, availability, and features of 12 crypto cards to find the right option for your spending needs.

Crypto debit cards are no longer just a way to convert Bitcoin into cash for everyday purchases. In 2026, there are plenty of options, from exchange-linked cards and stablecoin payment solutions to self-custody cards connected directly to crypto wallets.

But choosing one isn’t just about cashback. Availability depends on your country, better rewards may require staking or a paid subscription, and fees can quickly eat into your benefits. It’s also worth checking how each card works, as some are prepaid debit cards while others offer credit backed by crypto holdings.

We’ve selected 12 crypto cards worth considering in 2026, comparing their features, fees, rewards, and availability to help you find the right option, whether you prioritize low fees, stablecoin spending, cashback, or control over your assets.

The Coinbase Card Visa prepaid debit card that lets US customers (excluding Hawaii) spend directly from their Coinbase accounts.

It is a custodial card, meaning funds remain in your Coinbase account and are deducted when you make a purchase. Coinbase automatically converts crypto to USD at checkout or for ATM withdrawals, so merchants never receive crypto. You also get a virtual card as soon as you’re approved, with Apple Pay and Google Pay support.

Fees are relatively low. There are no annual fees or charges for spending USD or crypto, including USDC. Coinbase Card does not charge monthly or foreign transaction fees, and there are no Coinbase ATM withdrawal fees (though ATM operators may charge their own). The main cost is Coinbase’s crypto conversion spread. Spending USD or USDC avoids it, making them the cheapest funding options.

Crypto rewards are the main attraction, but rates vary. Rewards are optional, available to US users, and paid in a crypto asset of your choice from a rotating list. Rates are displayed in the app, can be changed at any time, and have no stated cap. Although many reviews advertise rates of up to 4%, Coinbase does not guarantee a fixed rate on its official card page. Check the current in-app offer before applying.

Best for: US-based Coinbase users who want a simple, low-fee card and crypto rewards without a credit check.

Crypto.com’s crypto debit card depends on your region. In the US, it is now a Visa Signature credit card issued by Comenity Capital Bank through Bread Financial. Cardholders can earn rewards in BTC or CRO, but approval requires a credit check. Applications are made through the Crypto.com app.

Rewards range from roughly 1.5% to 5–6%, depending on the tier. However, higher Level Up tiers require locking up or staking CRO. Those who don’t want to commit CRO can choose the entry tier.

Outside the US, the card remains a prepaid debit card. In the European Economic Area (EEA), the free tier offers no cashback, while the Plus plan (€4.99/month) starts at 2% cashback.

Best for: Users who already hold CRO and want to maximize rewards.

KAST is a Visa debit card that that spends a USD balance funded with stablecoins. It offers three tiers: Standard (free, 1.5% cashback), Premium ($1000 a year, 2% cashback) and Private ($10,000 a year, 3%).

Cashback is paid in dollars, rather than tokens. Monthly cashback limits apply: $2,000 in spend for Standard, $10,000 for Premium and $40,000 for Private.

However, KAST fees can reduce the returns. Foreign exchange fees on KAS crypto debit card range from 0.5% to 1.75%, while a physical Standard card costs $40 to deliver.

KAST estimates Standard users may keep up to 1% cashback after FX fees. Premium and Private also include a Visa Infinite card and KAST Points on eligible spending.

Best for: Fequent stablecoin spenders, especially those spending in USD.

Krak, Kraken’s money app, launched its Visa debit card in the US in August 2026. Available as a physical and virtual card, it lets users spend from 600+ fiat and crypto assets, with purchases converted to USD at checkout. The card is issued by Lead Bank and powered by Stripe Issuing.

Krak offers up to 2% cashback in USD or Bitcoin, paid after transactions settle, with no monthly or annual fees.

Rewards depend on users’ average asset balances across Krak, Kraken and Kraken Pro. The card is available in all US states except New York, Maine, Massachusetts and Indiana. Crypto-funded purchases may incur conversion spreads and trigger taxable events.

Best for: US users who hold a mix of crypto and cash and want one card for everything.

The MetaMask Card lets users spend directly from their self-custody wallet, without preloading funds. Built with Mastercard and Baanx (now Monavate) and issued by Cross River Bank in the US, it works wherever Mastercard is accepted, including online, in-store and through Apple Pay or Google Pay. Unlike exchange cards, MetaMask Card allows to retain full control of your crypto until purchase.

The card has two tiers: the standard card offers 1% cashback in mUSD, while the Metal card costs $199/year and offers 3% cashback on up to $10,000 in annual spending, no foreign transaction fees and higher limits.

Outside the US, it is available in Argentina, Brazil, Canada, Colombia, the EEA, Mexico, Switzerland and the UK. The trade-off is that users take full responsibility for securing their own assets.

Best for: Existing MetaMask users who want to keep control of their keys.

The Nexo Card is a Mastercard issued by DiPocket UAB, a Lithuanian-licensed e-money institution (EMI). It offers two app-controlled modes: Credit Mode, which lets users borrow against their crypto without selling it, and Debit Mode.

Cashback depends on Nexo’s loyalty tier, portfolio balance, and card mode. Rewards are paid in NEXO tokens or BTC, with payouts changeable at any time. Rates can reach 2% in NEXO or 0.5% in BTC, although Nexo’s regional page lists up to 2% cashback for credit card holders.

The card is available to eligible users in selected European markets, including the EEA and UK.

Best for: European long-term holders who want liquidity without selling.

The Bybit Card is a Mastercard debit card linked to your Bybit account. In the EEA and Switzerland, it is issued by Moorwand Ltd and Harmoniie SAS, with a 0.5% FX fee on top of Mastercard’s rate and a 0.9% crypto conversion fee.

Availability is limited to selected markets, including the EEA, Switzerland, Australia, Argentina, Brazil, parts of Asia Pacific, and Mexico.

Rewards depend on spending tiers or VIP status, not a flat cashback rate. The advertised “up to 10%” applies only to top tiers.

Fees and limits vary by region, and cashback without VIP status can be minimal, particularly outside Europe. US users should consider alternatives.

Best for: Non-US Bybit traders who want to spend without leaving the crypto exchange.

RedotPay is a custodial Visa card for global stablecoin users, allowing crypto spending in over 100 countries.

It costs $10 for a virtual card or $100 for a physical one, with no monthly or annual fees, and supports major assets including USDT, USDC, BTC, ETH, BNB and SOL.

Standard pricing includes a 1% crypto-to-fiat conversion fee for USD transactions and an additional 1.2% FX markup.

Standard RedotPay accounts offer no ongoing cashback, relying on welcome bonuses and referral rewards. However, its optional RedotPay Pro membership offers 3% cashback on eligible Apple Pay and Google Pay purchases.

The card is unavailable in the US, Canada, China, Japan, South Korea, the Philippines and Russia.

Best for: Freelancers and remote workers paid in USDT outside the US.

Uphold relaunched its US debit card in October 2025, advertising up to 6% in XRP rewards, with additional benefits for direct deposit users. Actual rates vary by tier and conditions, with some sources citing up to 4%. The US offers Essential and Elite plans, both paying rewards exclusively in XRP.

Essential has no annual fee but charges $4.99 for a physical card, $2.95 per ATM withdrawal, and $1.50 in foreign transaction fees.

Elite costs $99.99 annually, includes a metal card, and waives Uphold ATM and foreign transaction fees.

Crypto-funded spending may involve spreads and taxable events. Availability varies by state, while UK users receive a separate program offering 1% cashback in GBP.

Best for: US users who want a multi-asset card and are comfortable earning XRP.

Ether.fi Cash is a non-custodial Visa card that connects DeFi assets to everyday spending. Users can pay directly with USDC or LiquidUSD, or borrow against their crypto without selling it.

The card combines debit-style direct payments with crypto-backed credit. Rewards reach 3% in USDC for most users and 4% for VIP members on the first $50,000.

It offers up to 3% cashback in USDC for most users, rising to 4% for VIP members on the first $50,000 spent. Fees and eligibility vary by tier and region, with some benefits requiring ETHFI staking.

Best for: experienced DeFi users already inside the ether.fi ecosystem.

The Bitpanda Card is a simple Visa debit card for EEA residents. It is available in all Eurozone countries except Cyprus, and requires a verified Bitpanda account and the mobile app.

The card offers 1% cashback on eligible crypto spending, access to more than 600 crypto assets, no monthly fee and no FX fees charged by Bitpanda, although Visa mark-ups may apply.

There are a few important exclusions: stablecoins, precious metals, Bitpanda Stocks and fiat balances do not qualify for cashback. Each payment also triggers a trade, meaning standard trading fees apply. ATM withdrawals cost 2%, with a minimum fee of €2.

Best for: Euro-area users who already invest with Bitpanda.

Gate Card is a custodial crypto debit card linked to your Gate account, available in virtual and physical Visa or Mastercard versions, depending on region. Compatible cards support Apple Pay and Google Pay, with instant virtual issuance.

Cashback ranges from 1% to 8%, depending on your VIP tier or monthly spending. Rewards are issued as points redeemable for USDT, GT and other supported assets, subject to monthly limits of $5–$400.

Fees vary by card, but the latest Blue and AI versions offer free issuance and no monthly fee, with a 0.9% top-up fee and 1.5–1.7% FX fee. Their limits reach $150,000 daily and $1 million monthly.

Level 2 KYC is required. The international service excludes certain jurisdictions, including the US, where a separate Gate US Visa card is available under different terms.

Best for: Existing Gate users who want crypto spending with tier-based rewards and are comfortable keeping funds on a custodial exchange.

The best crypto debit card depends on how you plan to use it, where you live, and which features matter most. Before applying, consider these five factors:

Not every crypto card works worldwide. Some are restricted to the US, EEA, UK, or selected countries. Check whether the provider supports residents of your country and whether the card offers the same features in your region.

Look beyond monthly and annual fees. Crypto conversion spreads, foreign exchange markups, ATM withdrawal charges, and card issuance costs can all affect how much you actually spend.

Compare the rewards you can realistically earn, not just the highest advertised percentage. Some providers require staking, paid subscriptions, or minimum spending levels to unlock better rates. Also check whether rewards are paid in cash, stablecoins, or volatile crypto assets.

Exchange-linked cards generally rely on custodial accounts, while self-custody products can allow users to retain control of their assets until payment. Understand who controls your funds and what happens if your account or wallet is compromised.

Some crypto cards offer prepaid spending, while others provide credit backed by crypto collateral. These products have different risks, repayment requirements, and potential tax implications. Make sure you understand which type you are applying for.

Traditional debit cards use money held in a bank account, while crypto debit cards are linked to cryptocurrency exchanges, digital wallets or stablecoin balances.

When making a payment, some crypto cards automatically convert your digital assets into fiat currency. Others let you spend stablecoins directly or borrow against your crypto holdings.

The main differences come down to funding, custody, fees and rewards. Crypto cards can offer cashback in digital assets and easier access to crypto funds, but may also involve conversion fees, additional account requirements and tax implications.

In some jurisdictions, spending crypto is treated as a taxable disposal. It’s worth checking the local tax rules before using crypto for everyday payments.

Crypto debit cards make it easier to use digital assets for everyday purchases, whether through crypto-funded payments, stablecoin spending or cashback rewards.

But the benefits aren’t always as good as they look. Conversion spreads, foreign transaction fees, subscription costs and staking requirements can eat into your rewards.

The best card depends on how you plan to use it. If low fees matter most, an exchange-linked card may be enough. If you prefer greater control over your assets or want to spend stablecoins, other options may be more suitable. For cashback, look beyond the advertised rate and factor in all associated costs.

Ultimately, choose a card that fits your spending habits, location and risk tolerance. Check the latest fees and terms, and don’t overlook potential tax implications.

A crypto debit card is a payment card linked to a cryptocurrency exchange account or digital wallet. It allows users to make purchases at merchants that accept its payment network, with crypto or stablecoins converted into fiat currency when required.

No. Availability depends on the card issuer, payment provider, and local regulations. Some cards are restricted to US residents, while others support selected European, Asian, or Latin American markets. Features and fees may also differ between regions.

Some crypto debit cards have no annual or monthly fees, but other costs may apply. These include crypto conversion spreads, foreign exchange fees, ATM withdrawal charges, issuance fees, and paid membership subscriptions.

It depends on the card. Many custodial cards convert cryptocurrency into fiat currency when a payment is processed. Some self-custody cards allow users to retain control of their assets until the transaction takes place, while crypto-backed credit products may allow spending without an immediate sale.

Tax treatment depends on your country and the type of reward. Spending appreciated cryptocurrency may trigger a taxable disposal, while cashback and promotional rewards can have different tax treatment. Consult the applicable tax rules in your jurisdiction.

#5126. NYSE Taps Avalanche to Settle Its Planned Tokenized Stock Platform

NYSE Taps Avalanche to Settle Its Planned Tokenized Stock Platform

The New York Stock Exchange is turning to Avalanche to power the behind-the-scenes plumbing of its forthcoming tokenized securities platform, one of the clearest signs yet that Wall Street’s oldest institutions are moving core market infrastructure onto public blockchains.

Ava Labs, the company behind Avalanche, said Monday that the blockchain will serve as the “core ledger technology” for the clearing firm behind an NYSE-affiliated digital trading venue.

The platform, which the NYSE’s parent Intercontinental Exchange previously said it was developing, is designed to allow 24/7 trading, clearing and settlement of tokenized securities, subject to regulatory approval. Running on Avalanche, it aims to bring the clearing of tokenized equities onchain at institutional scale.

Tokenization, the practice of issuing traditional assets as blockchain-based tokens, has become one of Wall Street’s buzziest pursuits, but most efforts so far have centered on individual products rather than the market machinery itself.

The NYSE’s move is notable because it puts a blockchain underneath the clearing and settlement layer, the plumbing that finalizes trades, rather than just wrapping a single fund or stock in a token.

Dennis Lee, the NYSE’s head of digital issuance, said the exchange is “picking the right technology for each job,” and that while its planned platform will support equities listed across a range of blockchains, the ledger recording and settling trades needs to be fast, reliable and able to control who participates.

Emin Gün Sirer, Ava Labs’ CEO and founder, framed the deal as proof that blockchains can support not just products but the infrastructure they trade on, citing Avalanche’s “24/7 atomic settlement” and predictable costs.

The announcement adds to a frenetic race to put stocks and their underlying infrastructure onchain.

The NYSE separately tapped Blockchain.com to reach crypto investors with tokenized stocks and ETFs, while ICE and crypto exchange OKX are planning a 24/7 tokenized stock venue under an SEC exemption, and Morgan Stanley has moved to tokenize securities.

That OKX-ICE effort has hit friction, though: Every Magnificent Seven stock, including Apple and Meta, opted out of having its shares tokenized on the venue, with 67 companies in all joining them.

Research shows the appetite for tokenized stocks is real but uneven. An IMF study found tokenized stocks show genuine demand yet remain volatile and illiquid, a reminder that the infrastructure may be arriving faster than the markets to fill it.

#5124. ADA, XRP, DOGE: Gemini Names Biggest Losers of 2026

Crypto exchange Gemini has unveiled the biggest losers in the altcoin space for 2026 so far, as many of the crypto world's biggest digital currencies have been seeing massive double-digit price plunges.

Crypto exchange Gemini recently shared a list of 10 cryptocurrencies that have seen particularly brutal losses since the start of 2026 in a post on the X social media platform.

Topping Gemini's list of the biggest losers for this year is Bitcoin Cash (BCH), which has crashed a shocking 61.76% year-to-date.

Coming in second is Polkadot (DOT), with a 53.03% price decline, while Cardano (ADA) holds third place after shedding 48.04% of its value.

Ripple-related XRP and the top meme cryptocurrency Dogecoin (DOGE) are next in line, with losses of 43.43% and 42.38%, respectively.

Several other major cryptocurrencies are also seeing considerable losses, such as Sui (SUI), which has fallen 42.08%, Hedera (HBAR), which has dropped 38.13%, Solana (SOL), down 34.02%, Avalanche (AVAX), which has lost 33.40%, and Kaspa (KAS), which has gone down 30.75%.

Declining liquidity in the market, weakening speculative interest, lackluster institutional interest and general macroeconomic conditions are all major factors causing most major altcoins to underperform so far.

But for some, other issues within their ecosystems are also proving troublesome.

For instance, in the case of Cardano, the platform is seeing limited decentralized finance activity. As of Nov. 9, the total value locked in its DeFi ecosystem reportedly dropped below $50 million.

While Ripple continues its expansion in the institutional payments sector, stablecoin and tokenization areas, this hasn't necessarily led to sustained buying interest in XRP.

In fact, spot trading volume for XRP fell 53.5%, while perpetual futures trading volume dropped 44%.

Dogecoin, on the other hand, has been contending with waning speculative interest in meme cryptocurrencies.

Its institutional investment narrative also hasn't really caught on yet, with Bitwise announcing in September the shutdown of its Dogecoin ETF as the product failed to draw investor interest.

Bitcoin Cash has been seeing particularly sharp declines as it is now experiencing a reversal of its stellar 2025 performance, when the cryptocurrency soared around 38%.

Selling pressure picked up in May and June 2026 as BCH saw back-to-back monthly drops of over 30%.

A rebound could be seen in September after CME Group announced Bitcoin Cash futures, but this proved insufficient to stop the ongoing downtrend.

#5122. Bitcoin devs merge 3 new BIPs, and transaction fees could plummet

The Bitcoin proposals could cut fees by up to 27% through signature aggregation, with wallet upgrades and network activation still required.

Bitcoin's proposal repository added three draft specifications on Nov. 2-4 that could lower fees for payments assembled jointly by multiple users and for consolidating many inputs into one output.

BIP458 merged on Nov. 2, BIP459 on Nov. 3, and BIP460 on Nov. 4. Their mechanism combines the signatures attached to transaction inputs, the coins being spent.

Half-aggregation combines independently produced signatures without an interactive signing session: n signatures of 64 bytes become 32(n+1) bytes. Full aggregation requires coordinated signing but produces a single 64-byte signature for all aggregated signatures in a group.

For ten signatures, that means shrinking 640 bytes to 352 or 64 bytes, respectively. That's a 45% and 90% reduction in signature payload, before encoding overhead. The reduction in the whole transaction's fee would be much smaller.

Bitcoin fees are commonly expressed per virtual byte (vB). Under Bitcoin's weight rules, virtual size is transaction weight divided by four, rounded up.

Hypothetical examples put all inputs in one eligible aggregation group and use default signing settings, 34-byte output scripts and no optional extra witness data, or annex. Transaction overhead, witness encoding, and the group's marker byte are included.

A joint transaction with ten inputs and ten outputs would shrink from 1,016 vB to 944 vB with half-aggregation or 872 vB with full aggregation, lowering its fee by about 7.1% or 14.2%. At an illustrative 10 satoshis per vB, the savings would be 720 or 1,440 satoshis.

Consolidation benefits more in the second example of 100 inputs combined into one output. Its size falls from 5,804 vB to 5,012 vB with half-aggregation or 4,220 vB with full aggregation, cutting fees by approximately 13.6% or 27.3%.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

Ten signatures can shrink by 90% with full aggregation. Compare how much the whole fee could fall in two hypothetical transactions.

10 inputs, 10 outputs. Many outputs leave less of the transaction available for signature compression.

100 inputs, 1 output. Signature-heavy consolidation has a larger percentage fee benefit under these assumptions.

The input/output mix changes the fee benefit. These Draft proposals still require a soft fork and wallet support.

Hypothetical examples: all inputs eligible in one group, default sighashes, no annex, 34-byte output scripts. Blue includes the group marker in transaction panels; top section is signature payload alone. Not current network averages or available discounts. Activation parameters remain unset.

Original calculations from BIP460 witness forms and BIP141/144 encoding. Base=10+41n+43m bytes; W0=4B+2+66n, Wh=4B+2+2n+32(n+1)+1, Wf=4B+2+2n+65. vB=ceil(W/4). Bars scale by weight; labels are rounded vB. Fee savings=(v0-v)/v0 at a fixed rate, rounded to 0.1%.

Hypothetical examples: all inputs eligible in one group, default sighashes, no annex, 34-byte output scripts. Blue includes the group marker in transaction panels; top section is signature payload alone. Not current network averages or available discounts. Activation parameters remain unset.

BIP460 leaves activation parameters unspecified, and its proposed witness version, currently v2, needs coordination with other proposals.

Wallets would need to support the new output type, as existing Taproot outputs would not automatically gain aggregation, which is limited to direct signature-based or key-path spends.

Spends that execute scripts, known as script-path spends, cannot join aggregation groups, and eligible inputs can opt out.

Full aggregation needs a stateful two-round signing protocol, and reusing secret nonces can expose signing keys, making hardware and air-gapped support demanding. Half-aggregation avoids that joint signing session, although collaborative transaction construction still requires agreement.

#5118. Tom Lee’s Bitmine Adds $20M in Ethereum, Seizing on Vitalik’s 'ETH Is Money' Declaration

Tom Lee’s Bitmine Adds $20M in Ethereum, Seizing on Vitalik’s 'ETH Is Money' Declaration

Bitmine Immersion Technologies extended its weekly Ethereum buying streak, adding 9,587 ETH worth roughly $20 million, as chairman Tom Lee pointed to a recent declaration by Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin as validation of the firm’s accumulation strategy.

The NYSE-listed company said Monday its holdings reached 6,070,092 ETH, worth roughly $13.6 billion at a reference price of $2,234 per coin.

That’s 4.97% of Ethereum’s 122.2 million token supply, leaving Bitmine, by its own count, 99% of the way to its goal of owning 5% of the network, a target it calls the “Alchemy of 5%,” reached in just 15 months.

The firm has bought Ethereum every week since launching its treasury strategy on June 30, 2025.

Lee leaned heavily on comments Buterin made at the Devcon conference in Mumbai, where the Ethereum founder said, “I’ve finally made up my mind, ETH is a store of value, ETH is money.” Lee called the declaration “a seismic shift,” arguing it clarifies how ETH will capture value in what he described as a coming “supercycle of tokenization and AI.” (Disclosure: Tom Lee is one of several investors in Dastan, Decrypt’s parent company.)

He contended ETH functions as “pristine and trusted collateral” rather than a cash-flow token, reflecting its position as the layer-1 network favored by financial institutions moving assets onchain.

Buterin elaborated in an X post, laying out why he now sees ETH primarily as a store of value, including his view that fee-burning is weaker than it once seemed and that optimizing for it can conflict with decentralization. Lee framed that reasoning as consistent with Ethereum’s roadmap and its role in the tokenization wave.

Counting cash, other tokens and “moonshot” investments, Bitmine’s total holdings stood at $14.3 billion as of Nov. 8, including 216 Bitcoin, $475 million in cash and marketable securities, a $180 million stake in Beast Industries and a $75 million position in Eightco Holdings, which it bills as a rare public proxy for indirect OpenAI exposure.

Bitmine remains the world’s largest Ethereum treasury and the second-largest crypto treasury overall, trailing only Michael Saylor’s Strategy, which the release said holds about 849,327 BTC. Bitmine has 5,227,037 ETH, roughly 86% of its holdings, staked through its MAVAN platform, with projected annualized staking revenue of about $359 million.

The firm also recently argued the muted market reaction to the record Binance hack showed crypto’s growing resilience.

#5115. Solana perp volume explodes 65% amid shrinking spot activity

GMTrade accounted for 77% of Solana’s tracked weekly perpetuals volume, as trading incentives raise questions about sustained demand.

Solana’s jump in tracked perpetuals trading is concentrated at GMTrade, a derivatives venue that accounts for about 77% of reported weekly turnover in DefiLlama’s Solana table.

Spot trading moved the other way, leaving a narrower demand picture than the derivatives headline suggests.

In the Nov. 10 snapshot, the Solana dashboard reports nearly $9 billion in seven-day perpetuals turnover, up 65.29% from the preceding week. GMTrade, listed by DefiLlama as GMX Solana, contributed nearly $7 billion, with a displayed weekly increase of 105%.

Over the same dashboard’s seven-day comparisons, spot decentralized exchange turnover fell 5% to $9.9 billion, and stablecoin market capitalization fell 4.38% to $14.5 billion.

November 10, 2026 · DefiLlama’s Solana classification. Pacifica’s Off Chain turnover is excluded. Perps turnover rose as spot turnover and stablecoin market capitalization fell.

GMTrade’s share of seven-day reported trade notional, calculated from rounded displayed totals.

GMTrade’s share of outstanding notional at the November 10 snapshot. This is a level, not a weekly growth rate.

Turnover counts trades; open interest counts outstanding notional. Neither measures fresh collateral or new SOL buying.

Shares use DefiLlama’s Solana classification, excluding Pacifica turnover classified Off Chain. Perps cover multiple underlying markets. Turnover is a seven-day flow; open interest and stablecoin market capitalization are stocks. The snapshot establishes no weekly open-interest trend or incentive causation.

Captured November 10, 2026 around 05:11 UTC. Rings use GMTrade/table ratios: 6.919/8.958 = 77.24% turnover; 174.87/235.13 = 74.37% open interest. Whole-percent labels are approximate, derived from rounded displayed inputs. The up arrow indicates direction only, not magnitude.

Shares use DefiLlama’s Solana classification, excluding Pacifica turnover classified Off Chain. Perps cover multiple underlying markets. Turnover is a seven-day flow; open interest and stablecoin market capitalization are stocks. The snapshot establishes no weekly open-interest trend or incentive causation.

DefiLlama’s Pacifica page attributes its total value locked (TVL) to Solana but classifies its $2.6 billion in weekly perpetuals turnover as “off-chain,” a turnover outside the Solana table.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

Dividing GMTrade’s reported weekly turnover by the table’s total gives an approximately 77% share. The rounded totals and growth rates also imply that GMTrade’s weekly increase was roughly as large as the entire table’s gain.

GMTrade’s trading documentation allows SOL exposure backed by either wrapped SOL or USDC collateral. A dollar of leveraged turnover cannot simply be counted as a dollar of new SOL buying.

GMTrade’s GT documentation describes points earned through trading, liquidity provision and referrals, with conditional treasury buybacks offering a route to USDC.

The announced Season 2 terms tie a referrer’s sellable quota to GT earned through referred traders’ trading, so earning points is different from receiving an unrestricted cash rebate.

The published fee documentation lists basic crypto-position opening or closing charges of 0.02% or 0.024%, depending on whether a trade improves the balance of longs and shorts. The Season 2 announcement also described a fee-structure change.

The incentives don't show how much of the weekly increase they caused, or how much trading would persist without them.

GMTrade’s connection to GMX extends beyond the label. GM Labs said in a Sept. 16 governance post that GMX Solana had been integrated into GMX, but GT points remain distinct from the GMX token.

In a Nov. 3 forum reply, contributor Jonezee said Solana-side buybacks were adding GMX to the staking rewards pool.

The Nov. 10 perps table shows nearly $174.9 million in GMTrade open interest, against $235.1 million for the table overall.

Open interest measures outstanding positions, while turnover measures the notional value traded during a period.

A weekly trend requires comparable observations over time, while stablecoin market capitalization measures the price-valued circulating balance. Falling spot turnover likewise measures activity.

The next test is whether GMTrade sustains its turnover across subsequent weeks, with open interest observed over matching periods. That would help distinguish repeat activity from a single weekly jump while the broader spot market remains quieter.

#5113. Vitalik Buterin Moves On From ‘Ultrasound Money,’ Says ETH’s Value Is Stability

Vitalik Buterin Moves On From ‘Ultrasound Money,’ Says ETH’s Value Is Stability

Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin wants people to stop valuing ETH as a fee-burning token and start treating it as a store of value, arguing that the burn was "never" as strong as it seemed. Binji Pande, founding member of Ethlabs, told Decrypt that he agrees, claiming that it is a more "future-proof store of value" than Bitcoin.

It's a notable shift in messaging from the Ethereum community, after ETH was branded as "ultrasound money" when the network started burning a portion of every transaction fee in 2021. The argument was that as more people used Ethereum, fewer coins would exist in circulation, and fewer coins meant a higher price. Now, however, Buterin claims that isn't as important as people may think.

"We should not feel sad if fee burn is < 10% of breakeven indefinitely; the value of ETH comes from elsewhere," Buterin tweeted on Sunday. That value, he argued, is stability, which comes from Ethereum being decentralized, resistant to censorship, and having strong security.

Expanding on why I believe "ETH as store of value (SoV)" makes much more sense to focus on than "ETH as fee-burning token".

1. ETH as fee-burning token never was as strong as it seemed2. Optimizing for ETH as fee-burning token would demand Ethereum and…

— vitalik.eth (@VitalikButerin) November 8, 2026

Those in favour of the fee-burning structure argued that fees gave ETH a price floor, much like a company's stock may be backed by revenue and profits. From 2021 to 2023, Ethereum burned millions of ETH every year, but Buterin said this was "never a true floor." Most of these burns, he said, came from internal crypto uses like lending and silly NFT purchases.

Connor Howe, co-founder and CEO of infrastructure provider Enso, told Decrypt that Ethereum's layer-2 expansion made the fee-burning argument worse. Connor said that a significant portion of activity has moved onto layer-2s, and users pay "almost nothing" to settle transactions. To optimize a fee-burning token, he added, you'd also want to optimise for higher gas prices—which no one wants.

"If we decide we actually want to optimize ETH for burning fees to justify a present-day ~$270B market cap, then Ethereum would essentially be functioning as a world-scale major corporation," Buterin said. In doing that, Ethereum would lose one of its major selling points: decentralization.

"I agree with Vitalik, and I also believe that ETH is best positioned as a future-proof store of value," Ethlabs' Pande told Decrypt. He highlighted that the network is censorship-resistant, that it has "great distribution," is widely available, and that it is "at the center of future growing sectors such as tokenization, Stablecoins, and DeFi."

A store of value is an asset that holds its worth over time, meaning investors can put their money into it without fearing it will disappear. Historically, gold has been seen as the ultimate store of value, and Bitcoin supporters have pitched it as a "digital gold."

Pande, however, argues that both fall short compared to Ethereum.

"Gold has held the SoV role for a long time, but given the world we live in now is much more globalized, we need increased portability, something gold simply cannot offer," Pande told Decrypt. "Storing gold is simply not useful either past a certain point; you reach physical limits."

When it comes to Bitcoin as a store of value, Pande expressed concern that it isn't "future-proof" as AI and quantum threats loom.

Ethereum has been preparing for those threats for a while. In January, the Ethereum Foundation formed a dedicated post-quantum team and, more recently, researchers have debated the potential AI-powered threats. By contrast, the Bitcoin community appears more fractured on how to tackle the quantum threat.

"Security is ingrained more deeply into Ethereum’s culture than anywhere else," Julian Ma, co-founder of Ethlabs, told Decrypt. "AI being used for cyberattacks and potentially breaking cryptography strengthens ETH's position as a programmable store-of-value asset because the years of preparation and large investments into security and formal verification of the Ethereum protocol and its applications are about to pay off."

Pande added that newer assets can’t compete with ETH as a store of value, because there’s no way to verify they’re fairly distributed. The Ethereum Foundation owns less than 0.2% of ETH, he said, and the token has traded for 11 years.

“Trying to distribute or mine a new asset as a SoV in the lord’s 2026 is like trying to hide a sack of meat in a sea of piranhas,” he said. “It can and WILL get centralized.”

Not everyone thinks Ethereum’s leadership got here quickly enough, though. Howe said the Ethereum Foundation “needs to start to actually listen and look at the applications building on top of their network.”

“I think he is right,” Howe said of Buterin's recent tweet, “and I think it took too long to say it.”

#5110. Strategy’s STRC hit $100, but common stock still funded last week’s Bitcoin purchases

The November 2–8 filing reports no STRC ATM sales, while buybacks absorbed two-thirds of the week’s MSTR proceeds.

Strategy funded its Bitcoin purchases with common-stock sales during November 2–8, even as STRC, its variable-rate preferred stock, reached $100. Strategy had said it would not issue STRC below that price, but its November 9 disclosure reported no STRC sales under its at-the-market offering program.

The allocation shows where the week’s financing went. Of $283.8 million in net proceeds from MSTR common shares, $188.3 million funded STRC repurchases, $81.9 million funded Bitcoin purchases and $13.6 million went to USD Cash, according to the weekly financing update.

That put about two-thirds of the new common-stock money into buying back preferred shares. Less than a third went into Bitcoin. STRC’s market-price recovery had yet to become reported STRC ATM financing within that week.

Strategy’s November 2–8 MSTR proceeds went mostly to repurchasing preferred shares. No STRC ATM sales were reported for the week.

STRC buybacks received 2.3 times the common-stock proceeds allocated to Bitcoin.

Strategy’s July 27 policy announcement said the company would not issue STRC below $100. It also described a long-term objective of healthy, sustained trading near that level. The issuance and dividend policies were statements of intent that could change.

Strategy receives new equity capital when it sells shares and collects proceeds. In a secondary-market trade, a buyer instead pays another investor for an existing share. The market price and the issuer’s financing proceeds therefore describe separate transactions.

For November 2–8, the reported financing came from selling 2,051,348 MSTR shares. That share-sales figure includes shares sold but not yet settled as of November 6.

CryptoSlate’s November 5 coverage described renewed STRC financing as a possibility and included an unconfirmed tracker estimate that issuance might have financed 191 BTC. The subsequent weekly disclosure reports no STRC ATM sales for the period containing November 4.

Strategy bought 1,036 BTC for $81.9 million during the week, at an average $79,012 per coin including fees and expenses. Its Bitcoin purchase disclosure expressly identifies MSTR sale proceeds as the funding source.

Bitcoin purchases absorbed 28.9% of the common-stock proceeds. STRC repurchases took 66.3%, or about 2.3 times the Bitcoin allocation, while USD Cash received 4.8%. That makes the buyback program a substantial competing use of newly raised capital.

It also changes the mix of claims on the company. Issuing MSTR expands the common-share base, while repurchasing STRC reduces preferred claims. Strategy bought back 1,887,464 STRC shares during November 2–8.

The company’s July policy described discounted repurchases as a way to reduce future preferred dividend requirements. That gives the spending an economic purpose alongside its immediate cash cost. The eventual dividend savings depend on future rates and declarations.

The policy also anticipated a greater repurchase pace at deeper discounts, tapering as STRC approached $100. It left decisions subject to liquidity, market conditions and capital-allocation priorities.

The preceding week also paired MSTR fundraising with STRC repurchases, but disclosed a different funding route. In its November 2 filing, Strategy reported $164 million in MSTR proceeds: $65.5 million funded Bitcoin purchases and $98.5 million went to USD Cash. It separately used $208.2 million of USD Cash for STRC repurchases.

For November 2–8, the company explicitly assigned MSTR proceeds directly to the buybacks. The comparison shows a change in the disclosed funding allocation, with common-stock sales financing Bitcoin purchases in both weeks.

At November 8, USD Cash stood at $146.2 million, alongside a separate $4.77 billion reserve for dividends and interest. Strategy used $21.1 million of that reserve to pay preferred dividends during the week.

The next financing development depends on reported issuer sales and the use of their proceeds. STRC’s return to $100 provided a market-price milestone; the November 2–8 allocation shows that MSTR issuance supplied the disclosed money for both Bitcoin accumulation and preferred-share repurchases.

#5109. Solana Set for Another Tokenized Stock Venue as Formation Notifies the SEC

Solana Set for Another Tokenized Stock Venue as Formation Notifies the SEC

A new firm is lining up to become one of the first to trade tokenized U.S. stocks onchain, notifying the Securities and Exchange Commission that it intends to run a tokenized securities venue on Solana.

Formation Securities said Monday it had filed the notice to operate under the SEC’s “Innovation Exemption,” the conditional relief the agency issued in September that lets eligible tokenized stocks trade through permissioned automated market maker pools on qualifying venues.

Trading is expected to begin in early 2027, with tokenized shares of Exodus Movement, Forward Industries and Galaxy Digital anticipated at launch.

The pitch is to deliver the benefits of blockchain trading, 24/7 markets and near-instant settlement, without stripping away the rights investors expect from real stock.

Under the exemption, eligible tokens must carry the same voting and dividend rights as traditional shares, and each trade settles atomically onchain, a feature meant to cut settlement risk. A company’s transfer agent still keeps the official shareholder record, while every trade posts to the public ledger in real time.

“Tokenized stocks will succeed only if public companies see the value in them, and we intend to build this market together with issuers,” said Christopher Montagano, president of Formation Securities, which licenses its trading technology from Solana-based exchange Orca.

Tokenization firms Securitize and Superstate are expected to supply tokenized shares, with Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo noting its tokens are each backed by a real share held by its broker-dealer.

Access will be limited to verified investors, who can reach the venue through Formation’s platform and distributors including Alpaca, CoinList’s Passage, custodians Anchorage, BitGo and Fireblocks, and wallet providers Solflare and Squads.

The move adds another entrant to a fast-crowding race to put stocks onchain. The NYSE recently tapped Solana rival Avalanche to settle its own planned tokenized securities platform, Securitize launched tokenized stocks on Solana, and an OKX-ICE venue is also in the works, though every Magnificent Seven stock opted out of that one.

The effort builds on Project Open, a proposal a coalition including Orca, Superstate and the Solana Policy Institute submitted to the SEC in 2025.

Formation chose Solana, it said, for its speed, low costs and track record with tokenized assets, noting the network hosts more than $5 billion in tokenized real-world assets.

#5103. Maternal AI chatbot shows zero postpartum mental health gain

At 12 months, Rosie users showed no significant anxiety or depression advantage over a monthly children's book program.

Access to Rosie, an AI chatbot for maternal health, showed no statistically significant anxiety or depression benefit at 12 months compared with a monthly children's book program in a 400-person randomized trial.

The study, published Nov. 4 in JAMA Network Open, tested access to health advice among pregnant and postpartum participants with generally low starting symptom scores.

Rosie was designed to answer maternal and infant health questions, offer personalized guidance, and provide reminders and educational support. A clinical social worker reviewed distress alerts, contacted participants, and provided resources, so the intervention included human support alongside the chatbot.

Researchers assigned 200 participants to each group. The US study enrolled English-speaking people aged 14 or older who were pregnant or parenting an infant younger than six months and identified as members of racial or ethnic minoritized groups.

Enrollment ran from October 2023 through December 2024, with follow-up completed in December 2025.

The primary measures were self-reported anxiety symptoms on the GAD-7 questionnaire and depression symptoms on the PHQ-9 questionnaire. The 12-month analyses included 361 participants for anxiety and 362 for depression.

The adjusted difference between the chatbot and control groups was 0.10 points for anxiety, with a 95% confidence interval of -0.77 to 0.96. For depression, it was -0.23 points, with an interval of -1.05 to 0.59.

Both intervals include zero, leaving uncertainty about the size and direction of any underlying effect. The trial established neither a clear benefit nor equivalence between the approaches.

Two equally sized randomized groups were compared on anxiety and depression after 12 months. Neither primary outcome showed a statistically significant difference.

Anxiety: adjusted mean difference 0.10 points. The 95% confidence interval crosses zero.

Depression: adjusted mean difference -0.23 points. The 95% confidence interval crosses zero.

Reported adjusted chatbot-minus-control differences and 95% confidence intervals. Interval geometry uses a shared -1.2 to +1.2 questionnaire-point domain, scaled linearly; no effect estimates are recalculated. Each option represents a different symptom questionnaire.

Outcomes were self-reported. Rosie included incentives and human support; both groups could contact staff.

Participants received small payments for questions and book rewards for the most active users. The control group received one children's board book per month for a year, and both groups could communicate with study staff.

Those conditions limit how directly the findings translate to routine chatbot use.

Participants generally began with low symptom scores. Among those with available baseline responses, the median anxiety score was 2, and the median depression score was 3. About 11.1% and 9.5% of the participants, respectively, met the study's moderate-to-severe threshold.

Self-reported health outcomes are another limitation, and the authors urged caution about secondary and exploratory findings.

Rosie, delivered with incentives and human backup to this population, showed no statistically significant 12-month advantage on the two primary mental-health measures. Whether other AI interventions or different clinical populations would yield different results remains open.

#5102. Nvidia's $350 Billion Scare: What Happens If Jensen Huang Leaves?

Nvidia's $350 Billion Scare: What Happens If Jensen Huang Leaves?

Nvidia shares plunged as much as 8% Monday, briefly threatening to erase roughly $350 billion in market value as investors weighed the possibility that CEO Jensen Huang could lose his job over an alleged Chinese intelligence operation.

For a company that has become the face of the artificial intelligence boom, that's a rather expensive scandal. And the investigation is just getting started.

Nvidia's board opened an independent investigation following reports that Huang had a romantic relationship with a business executive whom U.S. intelligence believes was recruited by China's Ministry of State Security.

Huang acknowledged being unfaithful to his wife in a message to employees, apologized and pledged to cooperate. Nvidia's board said it had no reason to believe company secrets or U.S. national security had been compromised, and expressed confidence in his leadership.

The allegations have not established that Huang disclosed sensitive information or knowingly cooperated with foreign intelligence. Investors, however, are already confronting the possibility of a much bigger problem.

Nvidia's market capitalization had declined from roughly $5.78 trillion on October 6 to $4.51 trillion by November 6, according to YCharts. That's approximately $1.27 trillion in lost value before the board publicly announced its investigation.

The latest controversy adds another risk to a stock already under pressure from questions about the sustainability of massive AI infrastructure spending.

Nvidia also carries enough weight to move the broader market. A November 6 snapshot placed the company at 6.93% of the SPDR S&P 500 ETF, which tracks the market-capitalization-weighted index of major U.S. companies.

At that weight, a 5.6% decline in Nvidia alone would subtract approximately 0.39 percentage points from the S&P 500's performance, assuming everything else remained unchanged—which most likely will not happen, considering Nvidia is the main hardware provider for many other companies in the country, including some in the top 10.

Yet the S&P 500 rose 1.48% Monday morning, while the Nasdaq gained 1.87%. Other semiconductor stocks also rallied, suggesting investors were treating Nvidia's troubles as largely company-specific rather than abandoning the broader AI trade.

Huang has led Nvidia since co-founding it in 1993. Replacing him would involve considerably more than finding another executive capable of presenting a new GPU.

The 63-year-old CEO has built an unusually centralized management structure and maintains important relationships with customers, suppliers and governments. Analysts have questioned whether Nvidia has an obvious successor.

Chris Weston, head of research at Pepperstone, told Reuters he didn't expect Huang to resign. But if investors genuinely believed his departure was imminent, the stock's reaction could be an "absolute bloodbath."

Bloodbath or not, the stock would be exposed to a lot of uncertainty. After all, Huang is the man responsible for building a whole imperium. It would be similar to Mark Zuckerberg leaving Meta, Elon Musk leaving Tesla or Vitalik Buterin disowning Ethereum. Beyond pure market analysis, Huang is the name behind Nvidia and market sentiment can react to that.

Dan Ives of Yorkville Ives & Co. similarly described Huang as not replaceable, although he acknowledged the investigation could ultimately result in his removal.

An extramarital affair alone does not automatically justify dismissing a CEO. Unless the scandal becomes a reputational risk for Nvidia, it’s possible that Huang escapes the scandal and markets recover as long as the company continues being extremely profitable. The more consequential question is whether Huang's conduct exposed sensitive information, created security vulnerabilities, or compromised Nvidia's business interests.

So far, the company has announced no findings establishing such failures.

A resignation could trigger another sharp selloff, particularly without a credible succession plan. But there is no reliable way to calculate how far the stock would fall, or whether investors would ultimately welcome new management.

Nvidia has been in a sustained bullish trend ever since ChatGPT entered the market in 2023. That said, its weekly chart was already flashing warning signs before the allegations emerged.

The stock traded near $179.34 Monday, significantly below its 50-week exponential moving average of approximately $199.16. That indicator tracks the longer-term price trend while assigning greater weight to recent trading.

The breakdown is particularly significant because Nvidia had spent much of its recent bull market above that average. Its last comparable breach occurred during the 2025 correction and the pre-ChatGPT era before that in 2022-2023.

The stock had already crossed below that indicator before the scandal, meaning the investigation accelerated an existing bearish trend rather than causing the entire breakdown.

Based on the weekly chart, several levels stand out:

These are potential price targets based on technical analysis, and a recovery above $190, followed by a move back above the 50-week EMA near $199, would improve the technical outlook.

Monday's weekly candle also remains unfinished, so a confirmed weekly close below the indicator would carry more weight than an intraday move.

President Donald Trump has already defended Huang, describing him as an honorable person who has done an exceptional job for the United States.

"I think he probably has an excuse for it. You have to hear it," Trump told reporters Sunday.

The president's support could help Huang politically, but the controversy comes as Nvidia navigates Washington's restrictions on advanced AI chip exports to China.

Huang has repeatedly advocated allowing Nvidia to sell processors to Chinese customers. Allegations involving a suspected intelligence operation could complicate those negotiations, even without evidence that the CEO knowingly participated in espionage.

China's Foreign Ministry has rejected the allegations as baseless.

Congressional Republicans have also requested a private briefing on Nvidia's security procedures, with lawmakers seeking Huang's agreement to a briefing date by November 11, according to Axios.

Investors have another important date approaching. Nvidia is scheduled to report its quarterly earnings on November 17, giving the market an opportunity to assess its underlying business against the uncertainty surrounding its CEO.

The board has not disclosed when its independent investigation will conclude.

#5096. Bitcoin in Limbo as Stocks Rebound Ahead of US Inflation Report

Bitcoin in Limbo as Stocks Rebound Ahead of US Inflation Report

After a tumultuous week across traditional and digital asset markets, Bitcoin has returned to prices last seen in early August, when the cryptocurrency mounted an impressive 25% gain in a single month.

This time, though, $75,000 looks a bit different for crypto investors, now that BTC is on the way back down. So where does Bitcoin go next? Here’s what the charts look like.

U.S. stocks rebounded Monday as oil prices fell, with the S&P 500 up 1.9% and the Nasdaq Composite up 2.2% by 11:30 a.m. Eastern. Brent crude fell 4.3% to about $94 a barrel, giving the broader market a lift even as Nvidia shares slid in a move that spooked tech investors.

The Bureau of Labor Statistics is due to release the October consumer price index tomorrow. The report tracks what households pay for goods and services, and it lands after a stronger-than-expected jobs report pushed traders to price in roughly an 80% chance of a quarter-point Federal Reserve interest rate hike in December.

Bitcoin was up 0.7% at $74,559 after touching its lowest level in more than two months. The rebound followed a wave of forced selling in crypto derivatives, with a CoinGlass snapshot showing $1.2 billion in liquidations over 24 hours.

Exchanges liquidate positions when traders can no longer cover their losses with the collateral they’ve posted. About $1.04 billion of the total came from longs, or bets on higher prices. Bitcoin positions accounted for $324 million, nearly 87% of it in longs.

Bitcoin rallied from roughly $57,700 to $87,350 before sliding back toward $75,000. The coin is currently trading at $74,685 after an early dip to $73,667, with the week’s candle still open.

The 50-week exponential moving average, or EMA, sits near $78,250, above the 200-week EMA at about $69,790. Both averages give more weight to recent prices, with the shorter one responding faster.

Their order still favors the longer-term uptrend, though Bitcoin is trading below the faster average, and the gap has narrowed considerably, which increases the possibility of a death cross in the future.

The Relative Strength Index, or RSI, compares recent gains with recent losses on a scale of zero to 100. At 47.8, it’s close to the midpoint, with a slight tilt toward sellers and some distance from 30, the level traders typically call oversold.

The Average Directional Index, or ADX, measures how strongly prices are trending in either direction. The chart’s reading of 21.9 is below the commonly used 25 threshold for a strong trend, even though its accompanying directional indicators give buyers a slight edge.

On the way down, $74,400 is the first area where buyers could step in. Bitcoin has already traded through it this week, so a weekly close below it would put $72,550 in view, followed by the $69,000 area around the 200-week EMA.

On a rebound, the first area where sellers could stall the move is near $76,040, followed by the 50-week EMA around $78,250. A move through that average would put $80,360 back in play, with the prior rebound high near $87,350 beyond it.

#5094. Hyperscale Data (GPUS) Sells Its Last Bitcoin for AI Data Center, Months After Chasing $100 Million Treasury

Hyperscale Data sold its remaining 211.56 bitcoin for about $16 million by Nov. 8, bringing sales since late July to about 1,113 BTC as it races to energize 10 MW of AI capacity in Michigan in December.

Hyperscale Data has sold the last of its bitcoin, the NYSE American-listed company said Tuesday, unloading about 211.56 BTC for roughly $16 million to pay down debt and fund an AI data center in Michigan.

The sales were completed by Nov. 8 at about $76,000 per coin. Since it first sold part of its reserve in late July, Hyperscale has sold approximately 1,113 bitcoin for about $74.4 million, the company said.

That works out to an average of roughly $66,800 per coin. At today’s price near $74,200, the same 1,113 BTC would be worth about $82.6 million.

The company, which trades under the ticker GPUS, said the money will go toward its Michigan site, where it aims to energize the first 10 megawatts of critical IT capacity in December.

“We believe bringing that capacity online is our quickest path to profitability,” CEO Will Horne said in the release. “Selling our Bitcoin reserves reduces our exposure to Bitcoin price swings and gives us cash to pay down debt and fund the work in Michigan. We believe that’s the best use of this capital for all our stockholders.”

The exit is a sharp reversal from Hyperscale’s plans this summer. In late July, the company reported holding more than 1,106 bitcoin and said it wanted to build a $100 million digital asset treasury and reach full parity between its bitcoin holdings and its market capitalization.

“Every Bitcoin we acquire further strengthens Hyperscale Data’s balance sheet and expands our financial flexibility,” Executive Chairman Milton “Todd” Ault III said at the time. “We intend to continue building our Bitcoin position over time.”

A week later, Hyperscale disclosed its first sale, about 150.5 BTC, leaving it with roughly 958.5 BTC as of Aug. 2, even as its Ault Capital Group subsidiary bought about 15 BTC on the open market. The holdings were split between that unit and Sentinum. Ault said then that the company planned to keep a long-term position while tapping it for short-term flexibility, adding, “We are confident in our ability to use Bitcoin as pristine collateral.”

Tuesday’s release closes that chapter. Hyperscale said it still mines bitcoin at its Montana site and expects any bitcoin it holds on its balance sheet going forward to come “primarily” from its own mining operations.

Hyperscale is the latest public company to give up a bitcoin treasury in favor of data center spending. Keel sold its entire bitcoin treasury for $192 million in the third quarter, and Riot and Hut 8 sold thousands of bitcoin to fund AI buildouts and buybacks.

Larger miners are moving the same way. MARA said Monday it sold 13,597 BTC for about $1.1 billion after Sept. 30 and plans to keep selling to fund AI infrastructure, convertible note puts and share repurchases.

Hyperscale’s structure is more complicated than a pure-play miner’s. Its Sentinum subsidiary runs the data center business, while Ault Capital Group, a hybrid private equity firm and operating company, holds a mix of lending, defense, industrial and hotel businesses. Hyperscale expects to divest Ault Capital Group in 2027 and become a data center owner and operator.

The company said it expects to file its third-quarter 10-Q on or before Nov. 23. Because of a newly proposed exchange offer for its Class A common stock, it is also moving its virtual Investor Day from Nov. 12 to Nov. 23, when management plans to update investors on debt reduction and the December energization target.

Hyperscale did not say how much debt the bitcoin proceeds have retired so far or how much remains outstanding.

#5093. Coinbase, Kalshi and Payward backs CFTC in lawsuit over single-stock perp futures

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The crypto firms have asked the court for permission to file an amicus brief defending the CFTC's authority to regulate single-stock perpetual futures in the US.

A group of major crypto firms is seeking federal court permission to file an amicus brief defending the CFTC's authority to regulate single-stock perpetual futures in the U.S.

Coinbase, Kalshi, Kraken parent Payward, DRW Holdings, Jump Trading, and Paradigm have moved for leave to file the brief to support the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, according to a Monday court filing.

The brief comes in response to a lawsuit filed last week by the Capital Markets Alliance (CMA) seeking to overturn the CFTC's approval of the products. The CMA is an industry association with members including Cboe and Citadel Securities.

According to the Monday filing, Coinbase, Kalshi, and Payward have submitted applications with the CFTC to list equity perps tied to U.S. stocks, and the applications were deemed approved on Nov. 2.

Perpetual futures, also known as perps, are derivatives that allow traders to speculate on an asset's price movements without holding the underlying asset. Unlike traditional futures contracts, they have no expiration date.

In CMA's view, equity perps do not expire on a set date, so they should not be treated as futures but rather as "security-based swaps" that can't be listed on CFTC-regulated exchanges. The association claimed that the CFTC overstepped the Securities and Exchange Commission's authority.

The crypto firms, however, pushed back against that argument, saying the SEC was "at the table from the very start" of the regulatory process.

"The agencies jointly sought public comment on whether perpetual contracts should be classified as security futures," the Monday filing said.

Molly Abraham, general counsel of Coinbase, wrote on X that under the Commodity Exchange Act and the securities futures framework Congress established in 2000, security futures products are not swaps. Separately, Coinbase Vice Chairman Ryan VanGrack added that global markets are already trading equity perps.

"U.S. investors deserve access to these markets under the strong consumer protections and joint oversight of the CFTC and SEC," VanGrack said. "The U.S. should lead, not cede, this space."

#5090. Binance Hack Launderers Mint Elon Musk and Animal Meme Coins to Move Stolen Crypto

Binance Hack Launderers Mint Elon Musk and Animal Meme Coins to Move Stolen Crypto

North Korea’s hackers have found a novel use for the internet’s favorite joke tokens: laundering a slice of the record $6.4 billion stolen from Binance.

According to a report from blockchain analytics firm Elliptic, the launderers are creating their own worthless meme coins and using them to shuffle stolen funds through decentralized exchanges in a way that breaks the direct onchain link between the thief’s wallet and the one collecting the proceeds.

The launderers mint a brand-new token, pair it with a real asset like Near or a stablecoin in a liquidity pool, pay stolen funds into the pool through one set of wallets to buy the token, then sell it back through a separate set of wallets to withdraw clean-looking assets.

Because the pool sits in the middle, no stolen funds move directly from the buying wallet to the collecting one, and the receiving wallets look like ordinary traders cashing out a meme coin.

Elliptic, in research credited to co-founder Tom Robinson, said the launderers deployed the tactic on Tron and Near, with a close variant on Solana. The clearest example came on Near, where between Nov. 5 and 7 they created at least six home-made tokens, each paired with Near in its own pool.

The coins carried the usual meme-coin trappings: cartoon logos of a bow-wearing monkey, a flag-draped duck, a chimpanzee and a fox, one even featuring an “Elon 2028 President” image of Elon Musk, who has no connection to the tokens. The names ran from the crude (FUK) to a riff on dog coins (SINU).

Each token cost about 25 Near tokens, roughly $90, to create. All six followed the same arc: launched, traded heavily within hours using stolen NEAR, then abandoned, with the leftover liquidity pulled on Nov. 8.

In total, about 117,000 Near, or roughly $430,000, passed through the six tokens before much of it flowed into stablecoins or a cross-chain bridge.

The sums are modest so far, a reminder that the bulk of the haul is still parked onchain. Elliptic earlier found more than $3 billion of the stolen crypto had yet to be laundered, and that the launderers were spinning up a new wallet roughly every 25 seconds to move funds at machine speed.

Crucially, the meme-coin trick doesn’t actually hide the money. Elliptic stressed the trail remains fully traceable onchain; the maneuver only slows investigators down, buying time in what is essentially a race to move funds before they get flagged.

It’s one cheap, disposable layer among many that North Korea’s launderers stack together. Elliptic first observed the tactic last year while tracing the $1.5 billion Bybit theft, also attributed to the TraderTraitor group behind the Binance breach.

For compliance teams, the firm warned, funds arriving from a liquidity pool shouldn’t be assumed clean, especially where freshly minted tokens and a handful of trading wallets are involved.

#5087. MARA Sells 13,597 Bitcoin for $1.1 Billion, Says More Sales Are Coming

MARA sold about 40% of its bitcoin stack after Sept. 30, used $750 million to repay all its BTC-backed term loans and said it will keep selling to fund AI infrastructure, convertible note puts and buybacks.

MARA Holdings sold 13,597 bitcoin for about $1.1 billion after the third quarter ended and used $750 million of the cash to pay off all of its bitcoin-backed term loans, the miner said Monday in its Q3 shareholder letter.

The coins went for roughly $81,039 each. MARA held 34,012 BTC on Sept. 30, so the sale cut about 40% of the stack in a matter of weeks.

Repaying the loans returned 17,477 bitcoin that had been pledged as collateral, leaving less than 5% of MARA’s holdings loaned or pledged, the company said. Subtracting the sale from the quarter-end figure implies about 20,400 BTC left, before counting coins mined since.

The letter makes clear the selling is not over. “We intend to continue using bitcoin to build cash for purposes such as AI infrastructure opportunities, to satisfy the puts on our convertible notes that become exercisable next year, and potential share repurchases,” MARA wrote.

The board also authorized a buyback of up to $500 million of common stock after quarter-end.

MARA spent years building one of the largest bitcoin stacks among public companies. The shift away from holding came in stages. The company said it began permitting sales of mined bitcoin in 2025 and in 2026 expanded the policy to cover bitcoin already on its balance sheet.

It sold about 26,984 bitcoin for $1.9 billion in the first nine months of the year, including 3,891 BTC in the third quarter at an average of $75,300. It mined 2,247 BTC in the quarter and bought none.

Year to date, MARA has cut total debt by $1.4 billion, or 37%. It ended September with $946.5 million in cash and about $3.8 billion in cash and bitcoin combined, plus 1,408 BTC lent out that earned about $2.5 million in interest during the quarter.

MARA’s post-quarter sale fetched about $6,800 more per coin than bitcoin’s price near $74,200 on Tuesday morning.

The quarter’s headline profit came from the coins, not the mines. Revenue fell 35% to $164.2 million from $252.4 million a year earlier, even as energized hashrate rose 18% to 71.2 EH/s.

Net income rose to $632.1 million, or $1.28 a diluted share, from $123.1 million, helped by an $885.3 million gain on the fair value of digital assets.

Mining itself got slightly more expensive. Purchased energy cost per bitcoin at MARA’s owned sites was $39,674, up from $39,235 a year earlier, while blocks won rose 2% to 644.

“Bitcoin built the foundation. Power created the opportunity,” the letter opens, describing a strategy to “maximize the value of every megawatt” MARA controls.

MARA said it is targeting two data center leases before year-end but warned that “market conditions are fluid and that timing could slip into next year.” It received FERC approval for its Long Ridge natural gas plant purchase, currently authorized to sell 485 MW, and expects to close in the coming weeks. It also secured rights to develop a Matagorda County, Texas site with up to 2 GW of power capacity, and its Exaion unit signed a four-year, $79 million GPU-as-a-service contract with its first U.S. customer.

The move puts MARA in step with peers pulling away from bitcoin. Riot and Hut 8 sold thousands of bitcoin to fund AI projects and buybacks, Keel sold its entire treasury, and Core Scientific plans to exit mining by year-end.

MARA still describes bitcoin mining as its operating foundation, one that keeps sites earning revenue while higher-value infrastructure is built out. With roughly 13,600 fewer coins on the books and a stated plan to keep selling, the miner that once carried one of the largest corporate stacks is now using that stack to pay for its next business.

#5084. BitGo revenue falls 51% in Q3 as trading slows; net income rises on treasury gains

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The revenue decline was driven mainly by lower digital asset sales amid a continued soft crypto trading environment and a higher mix of lower-margin stablecoin transactions, the company explained.

BitGo BitGo Holdings, Inc. (BTGO) saw a notable decline in revenue during the third quarter of this year, which it attributed to weaker digital asset trading activity.

According to its press release, BitGo reported Q3 revenue of $2.85 billion, down 50.9% from a year earlier and 34.1% from the prior quarter.

The revenue decline was driven mainly by lower digital asset sales, which fell 52% year-over-year and 35.2% sequentially amid a continued soft crypto trading environment and a higher mix of lower-margin stablecoin transactions. After direct costs, revenue was $37.3 million, down 32.1% from a year earlier.

The company recorded an operating loss of $19.2 million and an adjusted EBITDA loss of $4.3 million.

"During the third quarter, we continued to strengthen BitGo's position as a leading institutional digital asset infrastructure platform," CEO Mike Belshe said in the statement.

Belshe cited the Go Network — the firm's institutional settlement and trading infrastructure — and its tokenized equity initiatives as some of the primary highlights in the quarter.

"We see significant opportunities to broaden access to U.S. equities through existing digital asset and stablecoin networks, while expanding the utility of traditional investment portfolios through on-chain settlement, collateral, and financing applications," Belshe added.

The company said normalized assets on the platform rose 32% year-over-year, reflecting continued institutional adoption when adjusted for price changes. It completed the acquisition of NYDIG's institutional trading business during the quarter and expanded off-exchange settlement connectivity, including with OKX. BitGo also repurchased $24.7 million of common stock under its $50 million authorization.

BitGo's NYSE-listed shares rose 4.94% to close at $5.10 on Monday and added another 2.9% in extended trading.

#5082. Google Takes Aim at Nvidia With New AI Chips Built for Privacy and Massive Memory

The TPU 8tx and TPU 8i Max join Google’s eighth-generation Tensor Processing Unit lineup, giving businesses four configurations for training and running AI models. Google says it serves nearly 100 TPU customers, from AI labs to universities.

Unlike Nvidia’s widely used graphics processing units, TPUs are purpose-built AI accelerators. Google developed them for its own services but increasingly sells access to outside companies, including on-premises customers.

“With computing moving from human to agent scale, businesses are asking for accelerators that can reason across AI models’ massive context windows,” wrote Amin Vahdat, Google’s chief technologist and senior vice president of AI and infrastructure, in Google's announcement.

TPU 8tx targets a problem that many serious businesses have when it comes to adopting AI: protecting sensitive information while a model is using it.

Data is encrypted in storage and during transmission, but processing typically requires decrypting it in memory. Google says the configuration uses hardware-level confidential computing to isolate customer data and proprietary model parameters even during operation.

The system combines TPU 8t silicon with fifth-generation AMD EPYC processors and Google’s low-latency Boardfly network. Google claims it can cut latency by up to half versus TPU 8t, with almost no performance penalty for the added protection.

Companies could fine-tune and run models on the same system instead of transferring sensitive information between clusters. However, confidential computing cannot prevent a poorly designed AI agent from mishandling data it is allowed to access.

TPU 8i Max addresses another limitation: memory. Autonomous agents analyzing data, codebases, documents or hours of video, etc; need fast access to far more information than conventional AI tasks.

Google combines two TPU 8i chips into one package, doubling computing power, on-chip SRAM and high-bandwidth memory throughput versus TPU 8i. The design packs twice as much silicon into the same rack footprint.

“Our fourth system, TPU 8i Max, is built for a different problem: the memory wall,” Vahdat wrote.

Google is open-sourcing TPU Sync, software that moves AI working memory between chips, servers and storage. It claims the system can reduce certain data-transfer delays and response startup times by more than 90%.

Google's announcement comes as Nvidia faces mounting pressure on Wall Street. Shares plunged 9.3% on Thursday and fell another 5% in Monday's trading after CEO Jensen Huang admitted to an extramarital affair with a woman suspected by U.S. intelligence of spying for China. Nvidia's board has launched an independent investigation, although the company says it has no evidence that corporate secrets were compromised.

The scandal adds to concerns about Nvidia's growth as competitors target its AI chip dominance. OpenAI is reportedly planning to purchase Google's TPUs to reduce its reliance on Nvidia. This is still unconfirmed, but Google's expanding chip lineup could give AI developers more bargaining power, threatening Nvidia's market share and future growth expectations.

Google is competing on specialized hardware and software, not just chip speed. Its open-source TorchTPU project also lets developers run PyTorch and Hugging Face models on TPUs without rewriting their applications.

Still, Google’s performance figures compare its new systems with earlier Google hardware, not Nvidia’s latest products. The company did not disclose pricing or availability dates for the two new configurations.
